#7296. Yahoo becomes first licensee of ‘Spotify-like’ collective AI content licensing model

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

AI content licensing has largely comprised of one-off deals between publishers and tech companies. RSL, which is being renamed The Internet Licensing Collective (ILC), is pursuing a collective model instead, with Yahoo as its first licensee.

The deal means publishers of all sizes can license their content for use in Yahoo’s AI-powered search engine Scout and get paid based on how that content is used.

Digiday spoke with ILC co-founder Doug Leeds about how this model works and how publishers can earn revenue.

A licensee like Yahoo pays a blanket licensing fee for access to content in the collective. Leeds declined to say how much Yahoo was paying, but the fee is tied to the “demand-side revenue.”  Yahoo declined to be interviewed for this story.

As a licensee’s business scales, the amount available for publisher compensation can grow as well.

“It allows businesses that are making a lot of money off of the content to keep that content in place and sustainable. But it also allows for entrants,” Leeds said. “As the revenue scales, the percentage scales. So more money comes out. But that also means that entrants can come in, and there can be new use cases and new players and new products, and all of that’s important to the evolution of what we’re doing to the market.”

A new report out today from publishing analytics firm Mather Economics estimated that collective licensing for AI could generate publisher compensation equal to 15% to 35% of AI company revenue, a potential market that in three years could surpass the U.S. display advertising market. The analysis was based on the current annual revenue from the two biggest frontier models, OpenAI and Anthropic, and an economic model created by Mather Economics incorporating AI training-data scaling laws.

Leeds likened the licensing collective to a Spotify-like model.

“It’s kind of like Spotify, but also a song is a song is a song, and content is very different. And so we had to come up with these rules that made sense specifically for digital content,” Leeds said.

Leeds said ILC worked closely with its publisher collective to figure out those rules. The first step was to define a “segmented royalty pool.” This separates licensing revenue by AI company, rather than combining payments into a single fund shared by all participating publishers. That means publishers only receive a share of the revenue generated by AI products that actually use their content.

More AI and tech companies are testing pay-per-value licensing models. Digiday has previously reported that Google and Microsoft are testing and developing these models, which compensate publishers when their content meaningfully contributes to AI-generated responses, rather than paying based on how frequently their content is used. It’s a shift from flat-fee licensing deals to more dynamic pricing, based on the value publishers’ content delivers to AI companies.

When an AI company signs a licensing agreement with ILC, that money goes into its own royalty pool. Rather than combining all these payments into one enormous fund and distributing them across participating publishers, ILC keeps the royalty pools separate.

This protects specialized publishers from having their revenue diluted, Leeds explained. A general-purpose AI search engine like Yahoo Scout might use large quantities of content from hundreds or thousands of publishers, but a specialized healthcare AI product might use a much smaller amount of content from a handful of medical-focused publishers, and that specialized content could be considerably more valuable to the healthcare company, he said.

ILC wanted to ensure that a publisher’s compensation reflects the value of the AI product using its content, not simply how much content it contributes to the overall licensing ecosystem.

“An AI vertical in health might be very low volume, but much higher specific value. So if your content gets used in that, you should split that high value, not get diluted by all the content that got used by Yahoo,” Leeds said.

Over 3,000 publishers are now part of the ILC, he noted.

The payments are determined by what Leeds called “allocation rules.” These take into account how much content is used, like grounding, retrieval or displayed in an AI response, and whether the publisher is credited or cited as a source.

For example, an AI product that uses a publisher’s content to check a fact might receive a lower allocation than one that displays detailed election results and explicitly credits the Associated Press, Leeds said. He did not disclose the exact formula used to calculate each publisher’s share.

The allocation rules are intended to discourage AI companies from choosing content simply because it’s cheaper, and instead incentivize them to use what they consider the highest-quality sources, he said.

Publishers can also decide who they want to license to. Right now, their only option is Yahoo. But as more licensees sign up, they can go into their ILC portal and select which AI companies they do and don’t want to license their content to. For example, if a publisher signs a separate content licensing deal with an AI company, they can take their content out of the collective. In the portal, they can also see what their content is being used for, such as RAG or training.

RSL, which stood for the Really Simple Licensing (RSL), was two things: a group of publishers working on a collective licensing agreement, and the organization behind the RSL Protocol, a standard that publishers can use in robots.txt to describe in machine-readable language what their terms of service are. Publishers can ask AI companies for a pay-per-crawl or pay-per-inference fee through the RSL Protocol, for example. “Thousands” are currently using this standard, Leeds said.

But Leeds wanted a new name for the non-profit organization that reflected the publisher collective, to make it clear that this is a licensing model that any publisher can join, he said.

Leeds said ILC is currently in talks with “some” of the major AI companies interested in the licensing collective. He declined to name which ones.

“You can do a deal with a one-off, and that’s fantastic. But that doesn’t invest in the infrastructure of content in general,” he said.

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

The IAB’s new AI attribution framework promises to standardize measurement, but a lack of platform data leaves much of AI’s impact invisible.

Ahead of TwitchCon 2026, brands and creators weigh in on if the platform’s big IRL event is worth the cost and potential safety issues.

#7292. Why user-first ad experiences build the strongest app businesses

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Today’s mobile users are searching, streaming, scrolling and shopping in bursts as they go about their day. According to RealityMine, the average smartphone user interacts with around 18 different apps per day, racking up to 200 app sessions daily.¹ In each of those moments, attention is won second by second — and lost just as quickly.

Capturing value in those seconds is remarkably difficult. When audience acquisition is expensive, and engagement is just as hard to earn, app studios cannot afford ad experiences that cause churn.

In-app ads are a core part of the overall app experience, yet monetization and user retention are still too often treated as competing priorities. The assumption is that earning more revenue requires heavier ad loads, which damages the user experience, while protecting that experience means leaving money on the table.

“Disruptive ads have a severe, compounding negative impact on both current engagement and future user acquisition,” said Scott Sheffer, vp of global partnerships on Google’s sell-side monetization team. “Disruptive ads not only drive current users away but also prompt negative reviews, discouraging future users from downloading the app. Seems intuitive, but now there is data to back that.”

According to a Deloitte and Google AdMob study, a single encounter with a disruptive ad increases a user’s likelihood to churn by 6% to 7%.² When those experiences repeat, 52% of gamers say they leave the app for good.³ On the flip side, high-quality, user-first ad experiences actively protect engagement: 72% of gamers keep playing when ads are high quality⁴ and 20% report playing more often.⁵ These metrics demonstrate how ad quality serves as a fundamental growth engine beyond a cosmetic choice.

“App economics are not an either-or,” Sheffer said. “With quality ad experiences, users can stay engaged longer and you can naturally unlock more opportunities for other revenue streams, like in-app purchases and subscriptions.”

Sustainable revenue growth doesn’t come from aggressive ad strategies that disrupt audiences. It comes from pairing global, premium demand with uncompromising quality controls. This foundation protects in-app experiences and grows long-term revenue opportunities.

To truly scale revenue, safety cannot be restricted to a single network. Google built its advanced ad quality protections to extend across the AdMob network and participating third-party bidders. This means studios can filter out unwanted categories, block problematic creatives and apply brand rules across their bidding partners from a single interface.

Toss, a financial app company from South Korea’s Viva Republica, chose AdMob as its mediation partner to grow revenue through quality ad experiences. By using AdMob’s real-time ad review and rigorous A/B testing capabilities, Toss grew ad revenue 891% year over year while driving a 17% uplift in next-day retention for one of its app’s features.⁶

Across the wider app ecosystem, SensorTower data shows that apps primarily using AdMob’s mediation platform have a 25% higher 60-day and 90-day user retention rate than apps primarily using competing platforms.⁷˒⁸

“Our focus on quality and long-term growth explains why, in recent research, Kantar found that app developers rank Google AdMob No. 1 in both providing high-quality ads and in maximizing revenue — ahead of other leading app monetization platforms,” said Sheffer.⁹˒¹⁰

As app development cycles continue to accelerate, monetization operations must keep pace.

Instead of losing critical hours to manual creative review, app developer teams can lean on Google’s intelligent brand safety systems to help. For example, improved AI models in the Ad Review Center can swiftly flag off-brand creatives for removal. In parallel, click-through-rate-based filtering helps to block deceptive ad behaviors like forced redirects, fake buttons or hidden close icons. This tactic helps developers eliminate these formats across all bidding sources before they trigger player frustration.

For ads that need a closer look, revamped search filters in the Ad Review Center help teams find creatives faster — such as sorting creatives by revenue to prioritize the reviews that impact the bottom line.

By protecting both developer velocity and creative safety, teams can more easily grow revenue and focus on the user experience. That balance of automation and control is why a Kantar analysis showed app developers rate Google AdMob 1.5x higher than the competitor average for making it easy to monetize apps.¹¹

Delivering on ad quality and making monetization simple is only the start. Google is building new tools to help teams catch performance anomalies, implement and validate ad placements and preserve player retention.

“We believe enterprise-grade AI and monetization requires secure, native integration across the tools you already use today,” Sheffer said. “That is why Google AdMob is continuously expanding what an advanced monetization platform can do. By shifting routine diagnostics, complex yield optimizations and brand safety into intelligent, automated workflows, we are bridging the gap between app development and ad operations — so you can build, test and ship ad setups with the same speed and safety as your app code.”

Partner insights from Google Publisher Connections

Publisher Connections brings together publishers, buyers and their audiences. Across a suite of trusted platforms — from Google Ad Manager, AdMob and AdSense, to their buyer solutions — they deliver continuous innovation and powerful results.

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

Marketers expect their 2026 holiday revenue to increase 1% to 31% or more in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue.

Local sports rights have gotten more complex and confusing as the regional sports network business changed. The leagues are now looking to simplify that offering.

#7289. Future of Marketing Briefing: Inside Guinness’ go-slow approach to creators

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of Marketing Briefing covers the latest in marketing for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Friday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

For a brand that takes nearly two minutes to pour, Guinness is in no great hurry where creators are concerned.

It has little interest in signing up creators en masse the way some of its rivals have. Rather, that side of its marketing will build organically, at the brand’s own pace, said global marketing manager and head of sport Paddy Carberry.

Speaking during a live recording of the Unofficial Partner sports business podcast in partnership with agency Sid Lee, the marketer made the case for why: “The minute we start getting involved in trends and social media stuff, all of a sudden we become the dad dancing at the disco.”

Few things kill a brand’s cool faster than chasing it — and Guinness has plenty to lose these days. Carberry traces much of the turnaround to the pandemic. With the pubs shut and most suppliers chasing unpaid bills, Guinness “extended our credit to all of our customers,” he continued, and it sent its unsold beer off to fertilize Christmas trees. Landlords and bar staff noticed, and that counted for a lot, since in Carberry’s view nobody has more sway over what gets ordered than the person pulling the pint. The brand’s ads played up the wait for the pub to come back so by the time the doors reopened, Guinness was one of the pints people had missed.

Guinness’ apprehension toward creators, therefore, is understandable. Its cool was earned behind the bar and a flood of sponsored posts could undo that work faster than a badly poured pint. Creators still played their part in the revival, mind. Most of them, however, weren’t on the payroll. London’s pint-rating accounts turned judging other people’s pours into a spectator sport and Kim Kardashian and Olivia Rodrigo did the brand plenty of favors without a Diageo brief. “Splitting the G” had been doing the rounds in pubs since at least 2018 when it earned an Urban Dictionary entry.

Over that period Guinness has nudged things along where it made sense. Its social team spent 2014 amplifying rituals drinkers had already invented and adding a layer of curated influencer partnerships. As Carberry tells it, the skill lies in knowing which moments to touch and which to leave well alone.

“While we lean into creators in appropriate ways we probably do less than you’d expect,” said Carberry.

And yet Carberry and his team seem more connected with how people view the brand than ever. Its “Singing Pints” campaign, which launched in 2023 but was revived over the summer, was inspired by the viral #SingingGuinness TikTok Trend where pubgoers poked faces into the creamy foam head of their pints and blew across them to make the foam “mouths” open and close.

“We took what was the consumer playing with the brand and amplified it into a TV spot,” said Carberry. “That was one of most successful pieces of content for the last 10 years.”

Creators are clearly part of Guinness’ future, just in a smaller role than the creator economy narrative would have it. Guinness seems more focused on community management and how creators fold into that. That puts it closer to where influencer marketing began. Before follower counts became a media currency, brands courted bloggers with tight-knit readerships and locals whose word carried on their own patch. The industry then spent a decade turning those relationships into a reach game. Guinness still takes part but it does so carefully.

“Guinness might not consciously be signing people up as creators, but ‘splitting the G’ has turned plenty of ordinary drinkers into exactly that,” said Jon Williams, founder and chief creative officer at Freedom Works. “They’re creating something to show their mates, in a pub, around a brand. That should be an ambition for any marketer: giving people something they want to do in real life, which then gives them something worth sharing on a screen.”

It also has less room for error than most brands. In the U.K., anyone drinking in an alcohol ad has to be, and look, over 25. The brand also can’t advertise anywhere where more than a quarter of the audience is under 18 and that’s a hard thing to promise on TikTok. The Advertising Standards Authority banned Au Vodka ads last year for exactly that, and Cranberry noted that advertising regulations are “stricter and stricter around the world.”

“While these results are above what we expected for this quarter, this level of growth is, of course, well below the level of growth that we expect from ourselves in the future… I’m encouraged by our progress, and I am proud of our team. But I want to be very clear, we are not satisfied.”

— The Trade Desk’s CEO Jeff Green when discussing the company’s quarterly revenues during the Q3 2026 earnings call.

34%: Average percentage of a brand’s video that TikTok viewers watch on the app

$3.4 million: Amount by which The Trade Desk’s revenue declined during Q3 2026

$34.1 million: Total CTV revenue that Nexxen reported during its Q3 earnings

14%: Percentage by which Nvidia’s stock dropped following the resignation of its CEO

OpenAI’s new business agents give brands a free way into the conversation it’s also selling as an ad

OpenAI now lets brands publish a free “business agent” in ChatGPT, the organic twin of the sponsored agent it’s been selling as an ad since September.

Why brands like Anthropologie are commissioning creator-created shows instead of posts

Brands like Anthropologie are paying creators to make original series, such as short comedy shows, rather than one-off sponsored posts, betting it builds lasting audience habits, sometimes with money moved out of traditional influencer budgets.

LinkedIn brings its B2B ad targeting to U.K. connected TVs

LinkedIn is bringing its CTV ads to the U.K., letting advertisers target streaming viewers by job role, industry seniority, though agencies doubt the premium pricing and targeting accuracy are worth it.

Reddit dangles seasonal ad credits but advertisers still want better proof it works all year round

Reddit is offering Q4 ad credits to win advertisers, but doubts about its measurement and ad tools make it hard to turn seasonal spend into year-round budgets, and slowing ad revenue growth and falling search referral traffic don’t help.

EU says ‘rabbit hole’ video feeds on TikTok violate its digital rules

The EU says TikTok’s For You feed breaks its digital rules by pulling users into harmful content “rabbit holes”, and it’s demanding redesigns, with fines up to 6% of revenue possible, per AP News.

Microdrama Platform Canvas Aims to Build a Netflix-Style Brand Around Creator-Led Shows

Canvas, a New York microdrama studio, wants to build a Netflix-style brand by fully funding short series made with creators, per Net Influencer. Creators get a 30% to 50% share of the IP, and Canvas makes its money from app subscriptions.

Just a quarter of U.S. adults trust creators to always disclose brand deals

A Morning Consult survey of about 2,000 Americans found only around a quarter of U.S. adults think creators reliably disclose paid brand deals, which points to a sizeable trust gap, per Tube Filter.

ByteDance reportedly plans a Muse-like AI agent within weeks

ByteDance is reportedly weeks away from launching a Muse-style AI assistant, according to The Next Web. Co-founder Zhang Yiming has reportedly told staff it may take until 2029 or 2030 to compete with Anthropic and OpenAI at the frontier.

#7285. AI is turning ad tech into a fight for the brain behind the buy

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The number of mergers and acquisitions in ad tech has been steadily increasing as 2026 draws to a close, proving an apt context for this year’s Digital Marketing Summit, hosted by investment bank LUMA Partners earlier this week, where attendees debated what’s most likely to spur the interest of corporate development teams

Opening the DMS proceedings were Terry Kawaja, LUMA Partners CEO, and Conor McKenna, a partner at the boutique investment bank, both of whom said AI is changing ad tech on two fronts: how marketing technology selects and delivers ads, and how consumers discover and buy products.

The first shift is in ad tech, with such technologies making previously complex workflows easier to automate; this movement is likely to prompt a shift in competitive advantage away from managing the process of campaign delivery to making better real-time decisions, i.e., firms that can better establish which signals to use, what creative to show, where to place an ad and how to improve results will thrive in the AI-first era.

“The workflow moat begins to evaporate with the AI technology that is coming out,” McKenna told attendees at the November 9 event, hosted in New York City.

This is likely to further intensify competition among demand-side platforms, supply-side platforms, agencies, retail media networks and major platforms over who controls advertising intelligence. Kawaja argued that efficiency matters, but effectiveness could matter much more. “I like to think of efficiency as the 10% incremental improvement, whereas the effectiveness could make a 10x difference,” he added.

For the open web to maintain its (modest) share of advertisers’ budgets — which the LUMA team described as fragmented compared with the scale and simplicity of large platforms — the challenge will be to deliver both easier workflows and demonstrably stronger outcomes.

The second shift is on the consumer side. AI assistants could bring research, discovery and comparison into a single interface, compressing the traditional purchase journey. The speakers expect people to remain involved for now, using agents to delegate tasks rather than handing over decisions entirely. If advertising becomes part of these experiences, consumer trust will be essential.

Together, these changes could reshape how advertisers attribute value, when it comes to the efficacy of their media spend, with several presenters at the event forecasting that such technologies will result in a more competitive market, as well as potential consolidation – a likely conclusion for investment bankers.

“Decisioning coupled with unique signal is what will differentiate the companies, separate the winners from the losers, and finally promote consolidation,” added Kawaja.

The key to success in such a scenario will be: scale, access to useful signals, decision-making intelligence and trust. However, the utmost metric will depend on whether platforms can show that their decisions improve results without undermining consumers’ confidence.

Speaking separately with Digiday on the sidelines of the event, Ana Milicevic, cofounder of Sparrow Advisers, described such technologies as: “Like this is the brain that we’re trying to outsource to a bunch of agents.”

Meanwhile, speaking separately with Digiday, Ravi Patel, co-founder and CEO of SWYM.ai, drew a distinction between setting budgets across platforms and picking individual impressions. Google, Meta and Amazon still make those impression-level decisions within their own systems, he said, describing unified media platforms, or UMPs, to quote the fast-emerging industry parlance. “It can manage the ‘what’ and the ‘how much.’ It can’t manage the ‘which impression.’ That distinction is the whole story,” he noted.

Local sports rights have gotten more complex and confusing as the regional sports network business changed. The leagues are now looking to simplify that offering.

Unsealed FTC allegations underline pre-existing questions over black boxes.

#7283. Customer Growth Manager at Uscreen

Uscreen is the video monetization platform for businesses building recurring revenue from video content. Our customers replace a patchwork stack of separate hosting, community, courses, email, and app vendors with one branded experience across web, mobile, and TV.

The platform is only half of it. We believe growth requires behavior, not just software. Customers don't stall because a feature is missing. They stall because they priced wrong, never built an email list, or launched an offer nobody wanted. Coaching is how we fix that, and it's part of the product rather than an add-on.

You own a segment, and you run it like a growth marketer.

We have a cohort of smaller video businesses where retention is not where it should be. Nobody owns it today. You will. The revenue that stays in that segment, and the revenue that grows inside it, is yours.

This is a coaching role for someone with a growth marketer's mindset. We wouldn't hire a demand gen marketer who couldn't analyze the state of the business and figure out what's needed, and we won't hire a coach who can't either. You'll look at a customer's business the way an owner would: where the revenue comes from, where it leaks, and what to do next. You pull your own numbers to get there. When a customer needs a custom report to answer a growth question, you build it yourself.

It's a one-to-many job. Your cohort is larger than a 1:1 book, so the work is designing coaching that scales: cohort programs, group sessions, office hours, and triggered outreach when the data says a business is drifting. You'll build that motion rather than inherit it.

Success is measured in dollars: gross revenue retention in your cohort first, expansion second. Not tickets closed, not calls booked.

A coach with a growth marketer's mindset. Maybe you've run growth or demand gen for a small business, built your own audience, or coached founders through their numbers. You understand how a membership business makes money, you pull your own analytics to see what's working, and you're comfortable doing it in front of a client. What matters is that you can find the real constraint in a business, then sit with the operator and say the honest thing.

#7282. Mattel brings Barbie, Hot Wheels to TikTok Shop

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This story was first published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

Mattel is bringing its toy box to TikTok Shop.

The company is launching a branded storefront on the platform as it looks to get ahead in live commerce. Mattel’s TikTok Shop store debuts this week with roughly 20 products across Barbie, Hot Wheels, Fisher-Price and Monster High properties. Mattel will soon expand the assortment to include more than 40 products.

Mina Zandbar, Mattel’s vp of global e-commerce, told Modern Retail that Mattel wants to use TikTok Shop for product discovery, engagement and education, alongside its traditional retail channels. Mattel has a TikTok channel with 124,000 followers — and TikTok users already discuss its products in videos and in comments — but Mattel hasn’t sold goods directly on the platform before. Zandbar said that, by being on TikTok Shop, Mattel can “be with our community where they’re looking for our brands.”

Because TikTok account holders must be at least 13 years old, Mattel’s TikTok Shop strategy is geared toward adults, as opposed to children. “Little kids are not going to purchase products for themselves,” Zandbar said. “It’s [more so about] education for their parents.” Mattel’s selection on TikTok Shop has therefore been curated to include products mentioned in existing TikTok content, such as the Fisher-Price Soothe ‘n Snuggle Otter.

Some of Mattel’s biggest properties already have TikTok pages with avid followings. Barbie, for instance, has 2.5 million TikTok followers, while Hot Wheels has 1.2 million TikTok followers. With its TikTok Shop store, Mattel can appeal to these various fan bases, while also grouping brands under one umbrella. The Disney Store, which launched on TikTok Shop this year, likewise operates multiple properties, like “Star Wars” and “Frozen,” as one storefront.

It’s Mattel’s hope that some shoppers will buy their first Mattel product on TikTok Shop. Then, as their needs change, they could grab additional products through Mattel’s website or retail partners like Amazon, Walmart or Target. In other channels, customers can access Mattel’s full breadth of merchandise and sign up for the company’s new loyalty program. “Over time, you build that relationship with your consumers, in a much deeper way,” Zandbar said.

Because TikTok Shop will serve as an entry point for some shoppers, Mattel is focusing much of its TikTok strategy on product recommendations. The company will create videos to answer common question like, “Which toys are best for a newborn?” Mattel will also invest in livestreaming and work with content creators who are embedded in the toy community and can speak to the company’s audience.

Still, not everyone visiting Mattel’s TikTok Shop page will have kids of their own. Although Mattel is known for children’s products, it also has “a massive community of adults” collecting items like Hot Wheels and Monster High dolls, Zandbar said. Amid what’s been dubbed the “kidulting craze,” Mattel will increasingly use its TikTok Shop presence to appeal to adult collectors.

Mattel is one of a number of major retail companies joining TikTok Shop ahead of the Black Friday sales rush. This holiday season will be the first winter that big names like Best Buy, Ulta Beauty and Sephora will sell products on TikTok Shop. Nearly a third of U.S. TikTok users expect to buy a gift this Black Friday, per TikTok Shop data.

Mattel’s TikTok Shop launch also comes on the heels of its new loyalty program called Mattel Rewards. Members can earn points on qualifying purchases and activities and redeem their points for discounts at checkout. The program includes four tiers, with benefits increasing based on annual spend. Already, more than 25% of Mattel’s consumers belong to the program.

As Mattel sees it, TikTok Shop and a loyalty program serve different, but compatible functions. With a loyalty program, Mattel can more closely communicate with its most avid fans and build relationships with them, Zandbar said. Mattel can also collect first-party data on everything from someone’s average order value to their location. TikTok Shop, meanwhile, helps with discovery, but it also keeps transactions and data within its own ecosystem. Both, Zandbar said, allow Mattel to “stay as close as you can to your community.”

Zandbar acknowledged that the holidays may be a hectic time to launch new marketing channels. But that can also work in Mattel’s favor, she said. “I just believe the busiest time is going to give you massive data to understand what is truly working or not working,” she said. “I want to make sure we are maximizing our learnings during the busiest time of the year.”

Mattel forecasts it will end its fiscal 2026 year in a strong position. For its third quarter ending in September, Mattel reported net sales of $1.807 billion, up 4% year over year on a reported basis. Mattel’s operating income totaled $476 million, up $96 million. Action figures, building sets, games and Hot Wheels saw gains, while Barbie and Fisher-Price saw declines.

Mattel also has a new CEO in Roger Lynch, who assumed his position in early November and is focused on driving growth at the 81-year-old company. “My first few weeks leading Mattel have reinforced my belief in the company’s potential,” he said in an earnings release. “I also see clearly where we must improve. … Our priorities are clear: strengthen our brands and win in toys, drive innovation and creativity, and invest with discipline to drive profitable growth.”

#7280. Playfly’s CEO explains why leagues are looking to consolidate local sports rights

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The gradual erosion of the power of local sports through the regional sports network system — brought on largely by cord-cutting that’s seen cable and MVPD penetration drop to well below 40% of all TV homes — has led many sports fans to struggle to find their favorite local baseball, hockey or basketball team.

That confusion, one would think, might lead to depressed ratings and a struggle to secure ad revenue for those respective teams. Not so, insists Craig Sloan, CEO of Playfly Sports, a sports rep firm that deals with 83 teams across those three professional sports, and is expanding into selling OOH, experiential and other media around teams and leagues.

Sloan noted the continued strong ad marketplace for sports, which formed the single strongest line of content to be sold in this year’s upfront marketplace. He said he also expects streaming to continue to increase its share of sports viewership as linear TV sees its share erode further.

The bankruptcy of Main Street Sports Group (MSSG) earlier this year forced 20 NBA and NHL teams to change their TV and streaming partners, and eight more changed their streaming home. Throughout, Playfly kept 100% of its rights and is selling local media for all 83 of its teams this season, landing local ad sales deals with several media channels, including TV station groups Sinclair, Scripps, Gray and Nexstar, along with sports streamer DAZN.

Sloan spoke with Digiday about the health of the marketplace and the impact of streaming on viewership and ad sales.

The following Q&A has been lightly edited for space and clarity.

It’s been a slow progression of how people have started engaging. The amount of people that are now watching through streaming for our local games across the country is not insignificant. It is in the mid to high teen [percentages] of total audience for any particular game, and we see a path in the not too distant future where over a third of the audience will come in through streaming. The remainder will be on traditional linear TV. That is all predicated on the idea that traditional linear TV looks the same in three to five years from now, which I would not be betting on.

The leagues [NHL, NBA and MLB] are more interested than ever before about consolidating their local rights into a centralized platform. Whether that happens or not — whether the teams will want that or whether fans will have a say in that or not — remains to be seen. It’s all being discussed at the present.

We have been in discussions with each one of the leagues individually about that potential and what they’re thinking that might be best to happen there. So no firm decisions. The NBA probably is the first decision because they’ve been talking about doing this in a consolidated platform potentially as soon as the 27-28 season, meaning next season.

The NBA is thinking about creating a platform with potentially one of the streaming partners to consolidate all 30 teams into one platform for their local games. So you would still only get your local game, but you’d go into one environment and you’d pick up your Knicks or whoever. The league hasn’t thought that they would have every team in the product, but they’re still figuring that out.

If they can get the majority of the teams to opt in, they can go to market with something that a that a very much a streamer would likely bid on, and then they can add other teams in if teams want to eventually opt in.

The teams might push back and say we want to keep it the way that it is, and they might win. The league might say we’re going to give up on the opportunity. But I think they’re clearly going down a path of exploring this potential option. Because everybody’s end goal is to create an environment where the fans know where the games are, it’s widely distributed so everyone has access to it, and engagement levels remain high, so that the the sport continues to grow.

We’ve kept the marketplace — advertisers and agencies — focused in on the importance of this space, and to not get caught up in the soap opera and the drama of where are they going to go? At the end of the day, the games will always be on. We’ve been able to still transact in the upfront process, [as well as] scatter, doing sponsorship level deals, [and] highly integrated partnerships — and nothing’s been disrupted thankfully. So we have not had a degradation of our narrative and/or our results.

We are sitting here on a weighted average because we have every team, so some winners, some losers. Every year, like an annuity, we get to bring out a similar, if not improved, supply. One of the calling cards for us in the marketplace is although the media rights holder might change, the fans are going to show up no matter what.

In general it’s been an okay marketplace, heavily predicated in the sports world — and probably overall —on football spending: NFL first, college football second, and then everything else in sports has fallen behind that.

The scatter marketplace never materialized … In today’s environment, you have to still count quite a bit on a healthy upfront to do well in a in a calendar year. From that perspective, we would still like to see more of a return to scatter. There were certain brands this year that decided to sit out the upfront and said that they just like the optionality of coming in in scatter, and that’s just starting to materialize.

AI certainly is, and we’ve got now this prediction marketplace. Sports betting is sort of settling in, but dependent upon what the leagues will continue to allow in their rulings for for what we can sell, that could be a very healthy category for us between Polymarket, Kalshi and others. Tourism and travel are back on the upswing as are financial services which are up significantly.

As AI automates advertising workflows, the contest is shifting to who can make the decisions that deliver results.

#7277. D+ Research: Marketers express revenue optimism for 2026 holiday season despite economic pressures

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This research is based on unique data collected from our proprietary audience of publisher, agency, brand and tech insiders. It’s available to Digiday+ members. More from the series →

This is an excerpt from our Digiday+ Research report The 2026 guide to holiday marketing strategies, including A-Frame Brands, Mastercard and Ritual, which examined which commerce channels are dominating marketers’ holiday strategies. The report is based on a survey of 90 brand and retailer professionals, as well as individual interviews with marketing executives.

In Digiday+ Research’s holiday report last year, brands and retailers expressed conservative expectations for their 2025 holiday revenue. This year, however, marketers are more optimistic. The majority of survey respondents (76%) said they expect their 2026 holiday revenue to increase 1% to 31% or more in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue.

Digiday also asked respondents if they expected their 2026 holiday revenue to decrease in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue. No respondents (0%) said they expected their holiday revenue this year to decrease by more than 11% in comparison to last year.

While survey respondents were optimistic about their holiday revenue expectations, some of the marketing executives Digiday spoke with said inflation is weighing on their minds.

“It is getting brutal out there,” said Ari Bloom, founder and CEO of brand incubator A-Frame Brands. “Costs are getting so high, and the economy is soft right now, and probably will get softer as we get into the fall. The cost issue is going to be really hard for a lot of smaller companies to absorb with heavy discounting.”

Bloom added that the political calendar may play a role in holiday spending as well. “When you’re in an election year, you’ve got a different dynamic because there’s a lot of discussion around inflation,” Bloom said. “People will have a sentiment of whether the environment is inflationary, whether things are too expensive or there’s an affordability crisis.”

Laura Brodie, chief growth officer at supplement company Ritual, said she too is concerned about consumer confidence but remains optimistic overall. “Even with the state of the economy and consumer sentiment sitting at a pretty low point in history, I’m bullish,” Brodie said. “Spending is resilient. It’s less about what happens in holiday spending growth year over year, which will probably be modest. Where the spend actually gets allocated will be interesting to see.”

Brodie said she expects consumers to spend more on experiences this holiday season. “We’re seeing a big cultural shift towards nostalgia, being intentional and spending time with family,” she said. “I anticipate people will gravitate towards gifts as experiences or things that feel personal rather than mass purchased. That includes brands that have a distinct point of view, or are mission driven or founder-led. Especially in health and wellness, brands that really speak to a consumer will have a strong opportunity to win.”

While survey respondents expressed optimistic revenue expectations for the 2026 holiday season, the majority of brands and retailers said they don’t have plans to offer deeper holiday discounts this year compared to last year. Eighty-one percent of brand and retailer pros said that they expect their 2026 holiday discounts to be about the same as their 2025 discounts.

Instead of offering deeper discounts, some brands and retailers are turning to other promotional strategies like bundling products or adding a gift with purchase to entice customers. A-Frame’s Bloom said that economic pressures, including higher transportation and fuel costs, present an additional challenge for the 2026 holiday season.

“This year will be more sensitive than last year because the economic situation is more difficult and prices are higher,” he said. “We’ve been talking a lot about bundling versus discounting because holiday is a time when people will buy multiples. They might buy the same gift for three different people, and they may buy it for themselves to try on top of it.”

Bloom added that offering a gift with a purchase can encourage holiday shoppers to become repeat buyers. “We recommend products, even if it’s trial sizing, that are able to be thrown in a bag,” Bloom said. “It is a really good way to get people who are buying a gift to keep something for themselves as part of the transaction. There’s ways for brands to think about getting more value out to the customer.”

Ritual’s Brodie said offering perks to existing customers during the holiday season encourages brand loyalty and can temper the expense of new customer acquisition. “Offering exclusivity to existing customers and to pull people back with something that feels unique, a compelling gift with purchase or seasonal drop.  … The cost of acquisition is high during holiday, so being able to tap an existing audience can be cost-effective.”

She explained that Ritual has also partnered with other wellness brands to cross-promote products and provide a gift with purchase during the holidays. “Brands that are in similar sizes can come together and create something that feels compelling that can benefit both brands,” Brodie said. “We’ve partnered with like-minded brands like [skincare brand] Osea and swapped gift with purchases.”

Susan Grossman, evp of consumer acquisition and engagement services at Mastercard said in an email that she expects brands to invest in rewards, exclusive access and experiences that extend beyond the holidays this year. “The more sustainable approach is to think about value more broadly. That could be a personalized offer, exclusive access, relevant recommendations, a seamless experience, or an incentive timed to the right moment,” Grossman said.

“But to do that, you need better signals and smart personalization,” she added. “When brands understand intent, they can be more precise with when, where, and how they use incentives. Instead of blanket discounting, they can create value for the consumers who need an extra nudge while continuing to build long-term loyalty with those who already have an affinity for the brand.”

#7275. The future of CTV lies in standardization

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Jessica Cobbe, svp, product – supply platform, Nexxen

For years, advertisers have focused primarily on in-stream video in CTV, treating the smart TV home screen as secondary real estate. But that dynamic is quickly changing, as this year’s NewFronts made clear. As viewers spend more time navigating content recommendations and streaming apps before pressing play, the home screen is becoming one of the highest-attention environments in media. The challenge here is that the infrastructure surrounding it still feels stuck in the past, lacking both nuance and standardization.

As CTV enters its next phase of growth, however, standardization will become one of the most important priorities shaping its future.

Unlike other digital or programmatic channels, native CTV inventory (including the home screen) has historically varied widely across device manufacturers, from creative specifications and ad formats to click actions and activation workflows. In practice, this means advertisers and agencies are forced to navigate a patchwork of technical requirements to achieve scaled reach. Consequently, adoption has been relatively slow, not because marketers question the value of this inventory, but because operational complexity often outweighs the benefits.

Without standardized industry specifications, preparing one native CTV campaign for multiple OEM environments can easily require 10 to 30-plus hours of prelaunch adaptation. For a single native CTV campaign, advertisers and creative teams may need to adapt the same asset package across 10 to 15 or more unique placements before launch. When dimensions, UI requirements, copy limits, image recomposition, QA and more complex executions like animated or pause ads all vary by environment, that work can add up quickly.

Worse yet, inconsistency impacts creative quality. The more time teams spend reworking assets for each environment, the harder it becomes to maintain a unified brand experience across touchpoints. Campaigns perform best when creative is designed intentionally for a given environment while still delivering consistency across the broader media plan, and standardization helps create the technical foundation that makes this possible.

Fragmented infrastructure also creates barriers to broader programmatic activation. Many buyers and planners expect the same efficiencies from CTV native inventory that they already experience across other digital channels, like display (think: streamlined workflows and scale). When inventory requires excessive customization or manual execution, it becomes harder to integrate naturally into a broader media strategy.

Standardization is not about reducing differentiation among OEMs. Rather, it’s about creating the connective tissue that allows advertisers to participate more efficiently across the ecosystem, and that efficiency benefits everyone involved. Advertisers gain simpler activation and more scalable reach. Creative teams can spend less time adapting assets and more time focusing on storytelling. Agencies can integrate native CTV inventory more seamlessly into omnichannel planning. And OEMs can make their inventory more accessible to a broader range of buyers.

As the CTV market continues to mature, operational simplicity is becoming just as important as premium supply. And for smart TV home screens, standardization may prove to be the unlock that moves this inventory from a promising opportunity to an outcome-driving media channel.Partner insights from Nexxen

#7271. ‘We’re not trying to be TwitchTok’: Twitch adds new short-form video feature

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

For Twitch streamers, on-platform discoverability has always been a major issue. Twitch has tried to address this, with mixed success. Now, the platform is making its biggest swing yet.

Starting early next year, Twitch will let creators upload short-form videos made outside the platform. Announced during the keynote at TwitchCon 2026 in San Diego, the move is aimed at improving on-platform discovery and creating more ad inventory. Twitch’s goal isn’t to make clips into a TikTok-like destination, execs say, but to help viewers discover creators and tune into their livestreams, where creators make most of their money.

The new uploads build on Twitch’s existing Clips tools, which have let creators and viewers clip livestreams and share them on-platform for more than a decade. But clips made or shared outside the platform are now a central part of creator culture.

“It’s a smart thing for them to do. You want to follow your favorite Twitch creators but you aren’t able to catch them live at that moment, so you’re going to TikTok or you’re watching old uploads on YouTube,” said Eric Bogard, CEO of UnderCurrent Talent.

Cross-platform follower conversion is difficult, especially for streamers. “Every data point will always show you that getting people to click over different platforms doesn’t work really at all,” said Tim McArdle, co-founder and chief content officer at creative studio Wildcatter.

Twitch’s chief product officer Mike Minton said the platform had “missed an opportunity” to make clips happen on Twitch. Historically, Twitch creators’ short-form content goes viral on TikTok, X, YouTube Shorts, and Instagram, racking up millions of views.

McArdle said that, though Twitch is a bit behind the game, these features could fundamentally change the platform. “They needed to do it, and for a company that produces a lot on Twitch, we’re excited about it,” he said.

Twitch streamers Vio and Frogan were less optimistic about the new short-form push. “People don’t go to Twitch to find clips or videos. They go to YouTube or TikTok,” said Vio. “They need to let the Clips thing go. No one is going to watch clips on Twitch natively,” said Frogan. Dan “Onepeg” Marshall, who has over 72,000 followers on the platform, said it’s a good move for the overall ethos of Twitch.

Twitch already has an existing “Clips” tab on both its mobile and desktop versions, but it will also add a new “Category Recap” shelf that will appear on category pages, which will populate with clips and recaps. Marshall found the Category Recaps the most interesting feature announced, noting it could be a positive for creators in specific categories trying to catch eyeballs.

“Of all of the things Twitch has tried to pull in the last several years, this is probably one of the better ideas for discoverability if it does more than just show clips of the largest creators in each category,” he said.

With this investment in short-form content, Twitch feeds will eventually become more personalized for users, whose viewing habits will dictate whether they’re fed more short-form content or more streams, according to Minton.

“We’re not trying to be TwitchTok,” he clarified. Instead, Twitch hopes these new features will help creators get discovered on-platform, which will make it easier to funnel viewers into livestreams, which is where they make their money.

“Will people consume as much of the clips on Twitch as TikTok? Probably not, but that’s OK… the utility of why they’re doing it and how it’s going to be used is different,” said McArdle.

Twitch’s new editing tools will make it easier to find exciting moments and turn them into clips. That could be a boon for smaller creators who don’t have the money to hire an editor or the time to edit content on an external app or program after they’ve been livestreaming for hours. Vio and Marshall wondered whether AI would power these features. Vio said the existing AI tools have been “ass so far.”

Minton told Digiday he’s focused on the “torso and long tail of creators” on Twitch. A more robust creator platform with better discoverability means more creators making money.

Allowing creators to upload externally made clips, rather than just chopped up, unedited versions of their streams, adds even more inventory to the platform. “It sounds like they’re trying to capture more inventory as leverage from an advertising perspective,” said Justin Miclat, chief growth officer at talent and brand management company Click Media.

Minton said increased audiences are what advertisers care about, and that Clips can help activations organized directly between brands and creators.

For creators however, the advertiser opportunity won’t yet translate into direct revenue. Minton confirmed that, though there are ads nestled amongst clips, there’s no direct monetization for short-form on Twitch just yet.

“Twitch is mostly live monetization, so the transition from the short form into live is where you’re going to make your money,” Minton said. “70 percent of it directly from your fans versus ad-supported. I do think over time we will find ways for ads in and around clips that’ll make sense that won’t diminish the user experience but will increase those earning opportunities for creators.”

Ultimately, this short-form push should help content creators on Twitch get more eyes on their livestreams, a notoriously difficult task the platform has been struggling with for years.

“Live content isn’t amazing for discovery… You hop into [a stream], you may not understand what’s going on. It could be a dead moment in a stream, it’s just not great,” Minton said. “Whereas short form is amazing…you get a snapshot of a great moment, and you can decide: ‘Is this something I want to spend more time on?’ Yes, it is. Follow. Boom. Next time you open up the app, that streamer’s in the feed.”

Yahoo is the first licensee of the Internet Licensing Collective, offering publishers a new way to get paid for content used in AI search.

You won’t just use AI tools: you’ll help design the agents, loops and feedback systems that make Paradox learn faster than anyone else.

-----------------------------------------

As Product Manager, you’ll be the architect of a new category, transforming growth into a living ecosystem powered by AI, behavioral science, and design.

You won’t just build features, you’ll craft experiences that rewire behavior.

You’re the architect bringing clarity between vision and execution, the person who turns “we should build this” into “it’s live, here’s the data and tangible impact.”

Protocols : guided frameworks grounded in psychology that rewire perception, emotion, and action.

Pods : human connection, accountability, and shared rituals.

Loops : adaptive, AI-powered coaching cycles that evolve with each user.

Personalization : experiences that feel uniquely theirs, every time.

Going from a successful personal brand, to an international tech-driven iconic brand, is far from easy. It will be messy, chaotic - requiring constant iterations on MVPs and innovation in tech. Therefore to achieve this here are :

-----------------------------------------

Translate the founder’s strategic vision into a clear product strategy.

Build and maintain the roadmap that connects business goals, user outcomes, and technological innovation.

Ensure every feature supports the evolution of the Paradox ecosystem: Loops, PX Agents, Protocols, Membership.

Be obsessed with user problems : talk to users weekly, analyze patterns, and identify where transformation breaks.

Turn insights from behavioral science, coaching psychology, and learning design into actionable product hypotheses.

Design loops, feedback, and rituals that make growth tangible.

build for both the heart (inspiration) and the brain (structure).

Apply Lean Startup methodology and scientific thinking to validate ideas through MVPs, not opinions.

Camunda is the enterprise platform for agentic orchestration, enabling organizations to coordinate AI agents, people, and systems across complex, end-to-end business processes. With built-in governance, auditability, and human oversight, Camunda gives enterprises the control they need to move AI from pilots to production — safely and at scale. Trusted by over 700 organizations worldwide, including 9 of top 10 US banks, Camunda helps enterprises boost operational efficiency, accelerate time-to-value, and deliver better customer experiences.Fully remote and global, we are in the middle of something bigger: transforming into an AI-first organisation, built on our own platform. We use Agentic AI to automate, orchestrate intelligent processes, and elevate human contribution across every team.Named GP Bullhound’s Top 100 Next Unicorn list, 2025 Great Place to Work certified. Visionary in 2025 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies. ranked 3rd in Flexa's 2026 Most Flexible Companies, We’re growing fast and looking for top talent to join our team. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

Camunda Hub is where enterprises run Camunda at scale. From Hub they manage their clusters and tenants, decide who can do what, and roll changes out across the whole organization. We’re looking for a Senior Product Builder to own the backend and architecture that let Hub manage a whole fleet of clusters. Your first focus will be the mechanism that sends access, secret, tenant and licence changes out to every connected cluster and brings deployments, health and audit records back. From there, you’ll shape Hub’s foundations: its event model, the service boundaries and data model other teams build on, and how Hub behaves when things fail or get upgraded. The platform is your product. Your users are platform administrators, Centres of Excellence and the builders across Camunda who depend on what you create. You’ll join a new two-person pod in our remote-first team. You’ll have real ownership as the person who makes the call, and you’ll build AI-first, from written specs through to production. Our customers run mission-critical processes on Camunda, so your work will matter to them every day.

Take over Hub's fleet propagation mechanism from the team building its first version, and make it work for access, secrets, tenants, licences and custom roles

Write short design documents for foundational changes, get them reviewed by the teams that depend on them, and turn them into tested, shipped code

Design how Hub behaves when things go wrong: offline clusters, partial failures, Hub outages, and clusters being adopted or detached

Build the path from clusters back to Hub, starting with deployment inventory, health, audit records and licence status

Design Hub's first event and webhook mechanism, so admins and agents can react to changes instead of polling for them

Join the pod's rotations and Hub on-call, and help other builders take on backend work through reviews, guidelines and contract tests

Experience building and running backend systems in production that other teams or customers depend on

Comfort taking a problem nobody owns yet and turning it into a design, a plan and shipped code

Product judgment for technical users: you talk to the people who use your APIs and make trade-offs on their behalf

Clear, short writing that lets a remote team review and decide asynchronously

Care for how a system behaves when parts of it fail, not only for the happy path

Designed and shipped a control plane or similar system that pushes desired state to many targets. Knows outbox or event-log patterns, idempotent apply, versioning, retries and drift reconciliation

Senior backend engineering in Java (API design, relational data models, multi-tenant SaaS services) and hands-on Helm and Kubernetes for self-managed installations

Software architecture and failure analysis: partial failure, blast radius, consistency trade-offs. Has debugged production incidents in a distributed system and is ready for on-call

Has shaped an API or platform as a product, and writes design docs other teams can build on. Gets agreement across teams without formal authority

Builds AI-first from written specifications and checks AI output as carefully as any other code

Kubernetes operators or GitOps tools, for example Argo CD

OIDC, SCIM and permission models, or secret managers and licence systems

Camunda 8 or another workflow or orchestration engine

Enough frontend skill to build a thin UI on top of a backend capability

#7237. Software Engineer - AI Tooling Infrastructure at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer, AI Tooling Infrastructure to join our AI Tooling team and help build and run the backend services behind Supabase’s AI products. The Supabase MCP server is one example of what the team runs in production, alongside the services that support agent skills and the dashboard assistant. You’ll work closely with the Platform, SRE, Auth, Billing and Edge Functions teams, and with the engineers on the AI Tooling team who build the product surfaces.

The AI Tooling team runs its own services in production and carries its own on-call. Traffic from AI clients and agents behaves differently from traffic from people: hundreds of calls a minute, retry loops, and no reading of deprecation warnings. This role makes sure the services they use are reliable, correctly authorized and observable. It is a systems and infrastructure role rather than a product AI role, and it is not an SRE role. You will build and operate these systems, not only advise on them.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments, owns what they ship in production, and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Build and operate the production backend services behind Supabase’s AI products, including the MCP server: deployment, scaling, configuration and release.

Take ownership of how those services are structured, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Define, measure and meet reliability targets for the services the team runs. Set SLOs with the SRE team, track error budgets, and use them to decide what to fix first.

Participate in the AI Tooling team on-call rotation, and be a first responder for incidents in those services. Investigate, fix, coordinate with other teams during incidents, and write the follow-ups and runbooks.

Work the AI Tooling team support queue alongside on-call, and turn recurring issues into fixes rather than repeated manual answers.

Own authentication and authorization for AI and agent clients: OAuth apps, scoped tokens, and permissions that make sense for agents as well as humans, including how we change or deprecate a permission without breaking existing integrations.

Own observability for the services the team runs: metrics, traces, logs, dashboards and alerts, so we find problems before customers do.

Handle capacity and abuse for agent traffic: rate limits, cost and safety controls, and behavior under load or when a dependency degrades.

Improve the architecture of the services the team owns as they grow, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Work with the Platform team on shared code, review ownership and release process, and with the SRE team on infrastructure, alerting and incident practice.

Contribute to the code of the AI Tooling team’s tools, such as the MCP server, where it affects reliability and security, in partnership with the engineers who own the product surface.

Have 5+ years of experience building and running backend services in production, and have carried on-call for them.

Write strong TypeScript or Go, and are comfortable working in the other.

Have designed and operated APIs with real consumers: versioning, error handling, rate limiting, and backwards-compatible change.

Know OAuth, OIDC, JWT and API authorization well enough to debug them in production and to explain where implementations commonly deviate from the spec.

Have a good understanding of PostgreSQL and relational databases.

Are comfortable with CI/CD, Docker and containerized deployments, and with infrastructure as code. Any tool is fine.

Can debug across services: follow a slow or failing request through several systems using metrics, traces and logs.

Communicate clearly in writing, work with a wide range of stakeholders, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly.

Pick your centre of gravity. We expect you to be deep in at least one of these, and to tell us which:

APIs and auth: You have designed permission models, token scopes or OAuth flows for third-party or machine clients, and you have migrated them without breaking users.

Operations: You have run production services yourself: carried the pager, written alerts and runbooks, used SLOs or similar to decide what to fix, and made shared systems less risky over time.

Gateways and distributed systems: You have built or run proxies or gateways, or debugged hard failures across services (retries, partial failures, upstream outages, fail-open vs fail-closed decisions), and you can reason about load and degradation.

Experience with agent tooling (MCP or similar), or serving traffic from AI agents and LLM clients.

Experience moving a service out of a larger codebase while keeping existing clients working.

Experience with Grafana, OpenTelemetry, Logflare or a similar observability stack, and real SLO work.

Open-source contributions, especially to Node.js, Go, TypeScript or API tooling.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#7230. Product Manager - Administration & Governance at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.About the Role

We’re looking for a Product Manager, Administration & Governance to join our Product Team and make Supabase the default starting point for software built inside a company.

Supabase already spreads from the bottom up: someone has an idea, creates a project, and builds something useful. Inside most companies, Supabase starts the same way: someone has an idea, creates a project, and builds something useful. The next step is making that momentum company-wide: one platform where anyone can start building, and where IT can see what’s running, who owns it, and what it costs.

You’ll own how companies run Supabase as their internal development platform: approved by IT and easy for anyone in the company to use. You’ll make it possible for people and the agents they work with to start building without waiting on infrastructure, while giving platform, IT, and security teams the controls they need to say yes. The goal is to make the approved path the easiest path, from an early experiment to a production application, with the controls companies need built in.

Own the product strategy and roadmap for internal innovation, from a company’s first IT-approved rollout to adoption across teams and business units.

Make getting started self-service, whether someone creates a project in Supabase, through an internal developer portal, or with an agent. Give builders the access and infrastructure they need without a ticket for every project.

Steer scattered usage into a manageable company-wide platform. Give companies one view of everything they run on Supabase. Shape how ownership, delegated access, and boundaries work so teams can move independently as usage grows.

Partner closely with platform, IT, security, and finance teams to understand what it takes to approve Supabase and keep it approved. Translate their needs into practical identity, policy, audit, and spend controls that don’t slow builders down.

Define the path from experiment to production, including project ownership, access, and the guardrails that change as an application becomes important to the business.

Work with Engineering, Design, Sales, and Customer Success to turn early customer rollouts into a repeatable experience, rather than a new custom integration for every company.

Define what success looks like, instrument it end to end, and let the data guide what gets built next—from time to first project and self-service adoption to production usage and expansion across the company.

Have 7+ years of product management on developer tools, infrastructure, or internal developer platforms.

Have built self-service products that developers love and platform or IT teams trust. You understand both the person trying to ship an app and the team responsible for how it runs.

Can reason about identity, permissions, organization boundaries, and usage attribution, and turn those systems into workflows people can actually understand.

Have a strong point of view on how agents change who can build software inside a company and what an internal development platform needs to make that possible.

Know your way around controlled experimentation and data analysis, and can connect easier adoption to sustained product use.

Are biased toward speed and would rather ship, learn, and iterate than perfect a spec.

Build with what you own and form opinions through first-hand use, from creating a project to configuring the policies around it.

Use AI tools to compress research, drafting, and feedback synthesis so you can focus on judgment calls.

Work async by default, making decisions in writing in a fully remote team.

#7228. Beyond Points and Perks: How Sephora Makes 46 Million Members Feel Known

A new retail battleground is emerging and it’s the AI assistant deciding what to recommend before a shopper ever visits either one. That makes loyalty more valuable, not less.

Emmy Brown Berlind, SVP and general manager of loyalty at Sephora, joins Matt Britton to explore how a nearly 20-year-old loyalty program is adapting to that shift.

Their conversation covers why the Beauty Insider program is designed to feel personal instead of transactional, and how Sephora uses AI without crossing into “creepy.”

She also discusses how its combination of first-party data, beauty advisors, stores, and emerging agentic shopping partnerships could help the brand stay relevant wherever beauty discovery moves next.

Emmy Brown Berlind is SVP and general manager of loyalty at Sephora, where she leads the Beauty Insider program serving more than 46 million members. She has spent more than a decade at Sephora across innovation, content, commerce, and loyalty, giving her a broad view of how the brand connects stores, digital experiences, rewards, creators, personalization, and emerging AI-powered tools.

Before Sephora, Emmy worked at Clorox and Yes To, experiences she credits with helping her understand brands at different stages and scales.

[02:47] When Experience is at Stake, Stores Still Win — Sephora’s physical stores remain central because the experience extends beyond access to products. Emmy points to highly trained beauty advisors, discovery-oriented store layouts, and sample rewards that let loyalty members try something new. The lesson for retailers and marketers alike is that digital convenience does not automatically replace physical retail when the store creates expertise, exploration, and value that online channels struggle to reproduce.

[06:07] Loyalty Has to Feel Personal, Not Transactional — As Beauty Insider approaches its 20th anniversary, Emmy says Sephora has kept evolving the program around choice. The birthday gift has expanded from a simple reward into multiple options, including points, because clients value choosing for themselves. That philosophy reflects the broader goal: loyalty should make customers feel recognized rather than simply rewarded for spending again.

[07:36] Personalization Can Turn Rewards Into Discovery — Beauty Insider Challenges use limited-time tasks and point rewards to encourage members to try something new. The tasks can be personalized based on purchase history. For example, prompting someone who has never bought a hair product at Sephora to explore that category. More than 30% of clients have participated in a challenge since the format launched in 2023.

[09:09] AI Shouldn’t Feel Creepy — More than 90% of Sephora’s sales come from Beauty Insider members, giving the brand a rich first-party data set. Emmy’s standard for using that data is straightforward: apply AI where it creates practical value for the client. Tools such as Beauty Skin Scan can improve shade and skincare recommendations, while the AI Beauty Chat remains optional. The technology earns its place by making the experience easier, not by showing off what it knows.

[14:31] Follow the Shopper Into the Agentic Era — Sephora does not expect every beauty journey to begin on Sephora.com. Emmy says the brand wants to be wherever people are shopping for beauty, which now includes AI-powered environments. Sephora has an app inside ChatGPT and a Google Agentic Checkout partnership that lets consumers discover and purchase without leaving those platforms. The strategy is less about protecting a destination and more about protecting relevance.

[18:12] Client-Friendly Decisions Are Not Always the Easy Ones — Emmy’s leadership philosophy comes back to a question she encountered early at Sephora: “Is this client friendly?” The answer can create additional operational complexity. Birthday gifts, rewards, and loyalty experiences require coordination across multiple departments, but Emmy argues that advocating for what clients genuinely want is part of the job, even when the simpler internal answer would be easier to execute.

Matt Britton is America’s leading expert on the millennial generation having consulted for over half of the Fortune 500 over the past two decades. He is the Founder and CEO of Suzy and the host of The Speed of Culture Podcast.

Playing it safe is expensive when nobody notices you.

Live from the Marketing Vanguard stage at Brandweek 2026, Jenny Rooney sits down with Tyler Stewart, head of marketing at Gopuff, for a play-by-play on how a challenger brand competes in one of the most crowded corners of commerce without trying to outspend everyone else.

Tyler gets into why Gopuff’s real edge sits in its first-party logistics, and how competitors can also become distribution partners

He also addresses why the promise of “instant” means more than shaving minutes off a delivery.

This episode is part of a special vidcast series that was recorded live during Brandweek 2026 and presented in partnership with Tatari.

Tyler Stewart is head of marketing at Gopuff, where he leads marketing across the business as the company continues to expand what instant commerce can look like.

His career started in sports entertainment and talent representation before moving through media and integrated marketing roles at GQ, The Players’ Tribune and Bleacher Report.

That mix of sports, media, entertainment and startup experience now shapes how he approaches Gopuff: find where the brand can create attention, build partnerships people actually care about and turn the company’s operational strengths into something culturally relevant.

[05:32] Could Your Competitors Be Your Best Distribution Partners? — Gopuff competes with major delivery platforms, but Tyler does not view the category as adversarial. The company’s first-party logistics allow it to sell through platforms like DoorDash and Uber Eats while also serving as a last-mile partner elsewhere. Here lies a lesson for marketers – competitive advantage can create more options than simply beating everyone else at their own game.

[13:11] How Gopuff Competes Against the Giants — Tyler is blunt that Gopuff does not have the budget to match every larger competitor dollar for dollar. His answer is resourcefulness. Partnerships with talent like Tom Brady are designed to create products, access, and culturally interesting moments rather than simply rent celebrity reach.

[14:32] “We’d Rather Be Debated Than Ignored” — At Brandweek 2026, Tyler offers one of the clearest statements of Gopuff’s brand philosophy. In an attention economy where being noticed is increasingly difficult, he would rather push far enough to spark debate than play it so safely that nobody notices. For a challenger brand, invisibility is the bigger risk.

[17:14] One Deep Creator Partnership Beats Ten Logo Slaps — Tyler’s partnership strategy is “do less but better.” Instead of asking talent for another sponsored post, Gopuff tries to build something with them, whether that means products, content, or unique access. The goal is to make the relationship feel organic to both the creator and the audience.

Jenny Rooney is Chief Brand and Community Officer, leading strategy for the overall ADWEEK brand as well as the ways in which we serve and support our audiences with high-value content, products, partnerships and experiences, notably through our community programs such as Marketing Vanguard.
